The '''2012 United States House of Representatives elections''' were held on November 6, 2012. It coincided with the reelection of [[President of the United States|President]] [[Barack Obama]].  Elections were held for all 435 seats representing the 50 [[U.S. state]]s and also for the [[Delegate (United States Congress)|delegates]] from the [[District of Columbia]] and five major [[Insular area|U.S. territories]]. The winners of this election cycle served in the [[113th United States Congress]]. This was the first congressional election using districts drawn up based on the [[2010 United States census]].

Although Democratic candidates received a nationwide plurality of more than 1.4 million votes (1.1%) in the aggregated vote totals from all House elections,<ref name="Clerk">{{cite web |url=http://clerk.house.gov/member_info/electionInfo/2012election.pdf |title=Statistics of the Presidential and Congressional election of November 6, 2012 |first=Karen L. |last=Haas |date=February 28, 2013 |access-date=May 29, 2013}}</ref> the Republican Party won a 33-seat advantage in seats, thus retaining its House majority by 17 seats. Democrats picked up 27 previously Republican-held seats, but most of these gains were canceled out due to Republican pick-ups of Democratic-held seats, and reapportionment gains that benefited Republicans, leaving the Democrats with a net gain of just eight seats.<ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.quorum.us/data-driven-insights/under-obama-democrats-suffer-largest-loss-in-power-since-eisenhower/291/|title = Under Obama, Democrats suffer largest loss in power since Eisenhower}}</ref>

This disparity — common in close elections involving [[single-member district]] (especially plurality) voting<ref>"...the discrepancy between the popular vote won by a party and the seats that party wins in Congress is an endemic feature of our political system." Trende, Sean. [http://www.realclearpolitics.com/articles/2013/05/16/why_the_gop_lost_the_vote_but_gained_house_seats__118430.html Why Republicans Lost the Vote But Kept the House]. [[RealClearPolitics]]. 2013-05-16. Retrieved 2013-09-15.</ref> — has sometimes been attributed to targeted Republican [[gerrymandering in the United States|gerrymandering]] in the [[congressional redistricting]] process following the 2010 census as part of the [[REDMAP]] project.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://www.contracostatimes.com/opinion/ci_23044745/timm-herdt-does-gop-now-own-house |title=Timm Herdt: Does the GOP now own the House? |first=Timm |last=Herdt |publisher=Contra Costa Times |date=April 17, 2013 |access-date=May 29, 2013}}</ref><ref>{{cite web |url=http://opinionator.blogs.nytimes.com/2013/05/02/house-of-un-representatives |date=May 2, 2013 |title=House of Un-Representatives |first=Timothy |last=Egan |work=The New York Times |access-date=May 29, 2013}}</ref><ref name="NYTGerry">{{cite news |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2013/02/03/opinion/sunday/the-great-gerrymander-of-2012.html |title=The Great Gerrymander of 2012 |work=The New York Times |first=Sam |last=Wang |date=February 2, 2013 |access-date=May 29, 2013}}</ref><ref name="GPalmer" /><ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.pbs.org/newshour/politics/gop-gerrymandering-creates-uphill-fight-dems-house|title=GOP gerrymandering creates uphill fight for Dems in the House|website=PBS NewsHour|language=en-US|access-date=2018-07-04|date=March 31, 2014}}</ref> "Unintentional gerrymandering," — the high concentration of Democrats in urban centers—leading to "wasted votes" in districts that easily elected Democratic candidates, has also been cited as causing some of the [[efficiency gap]].<ref name="Sides">{{cite news|url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/wonkblog/wp/2013/02/17/redistricting-didnt-win-republicans-the-house/|title=Redistricting didn't win Republicans the House|author1-link=John M. Sides|last1=Sides|first1=John|last2=McGhee|first2=Eric|date=February 17, 2013|newspaper=The Washington Post}}</ref><ref name="GPalmer">{{cite news |last1=Palmer |first1=Griff |last2=Cooper |first2=Michael |title=How Maps Helped Republicans Keep an Edge in the House |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2012/12/15/us/politics/redistricting-helped-republicans-hold-onto-congress.html |access-date=January 29, 2015 |work=The New York Times |date=December 14, 2014}}</ref><ref>{{Cite news|url=http://www.politifact.com/texas/statements/2013/nov/26/lloyd-doggett/democrats-outpolled-republicans-who-landed-33-seat/|title=Republicans won more House seats than more popular Democrats, though not entirely because of how districts were drawn|work=@politifact|access-date=2018-07-04|language=en}}</ref> The GOP also benefited from having a greater number of [[incumbent]]s, who tend to have an advantage in elections<ref name=GPalmer/> which may have helped Republicans win close elections for individual seats.

This marked the last of five House elections in which the party that won the popular vote was unable to receive a majority in the House. The previous four times were in [[1914 United States House of Representatives elections|1914]], [[1942 United States House of Representatives elections|1942]], [[1952 United States House of Representatives elections|1952]], and [[1996 United States House of Representatives elections|1996]]; in the former two elections, [[Democratic Party (United States)|Democrats]] won the House majority without winning the popular vote, whereas in the latter two, the [[Republican Party (United States)|Republicans]] did so.<ref name="NYTGerry"/><ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/the-fix/wp/2012/11/12/in-1996-house-democrats-also-won-the-popular-vote-but-remained-in-the-minority |title=In 1996, House Democrats also won the popular vote but remained in the minority (kind of) |work=Washingtonpost.com |date=November 12, 2012 |access-date=December 3, 2012}}</ref> The 2012 elections were also the first since 1996 where a president and a House majority of different parties were simultaneously elected, and are the most recent ones to date. Additionally, they were the most recent House elections in which a winning presidential candidate's party gained seats in the chamber.

{{As of|2026}}, this is the last time Democrats won a House seat in [[West Virginia]].

== Retiring incumbents ==
[[File:US House 2012 retirements.svg|thumb|350px|Retiring incumbents by district<br />{{legend0|#92C5DE|Democratic incumbent ran}}<br />{{legend0|#0671B0|Democratic incumbent retired or lost renomination}}<br />{{legend0|#F48882|Republican incumbent ran}}<br />{{legend0|#CA0120|Republican incumbent retired or lost renomination}}<br />{{legend0|#BB91B1|Democratic and Republican incumbent ran}}<br />{{legend0|#6D6D6D|Vacant or new district}}]]
Forty-one representatives retired. Thirty-four of those seats were held by the same party, six seats changed party.

=== Democrats ===
Twenty-two Democrats retired. Fourteen of those seats were held by Democrats, five were won by Republicans, and three seats were eliminated in redistricting.

==== Democratic held ====
# {{ushr|CA|6|X}}: [[Lynn Woolsey]],<ref>{{cite news |url=http://www.rollcall.com/news/woolsey_to_retire_after_two_decades_in_house-206843-1.html?pos=hln |title=Woolsey to Retire After Two Decades in House |newspaper=Roll Call |date=June 27, 2011|last1=Starkey |first1=Melanie }}</ref> was succeeded by [[Jared Huffman]] (with district being renumbered as California 2).
# [[United States House of Representatives elections in California, 2012#District 51|California 51]]: [[Bob Filner]], to [[2012 San Diego mayoral election|run for mayor of San Diego]],<ref>{{cite web |url=http://www.rollcall.com/issues/57_15/Members-Brace-for-California-Map-207765-1.html |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120106030542/http://www.rollcall.com/issues/57_15/Members-Brace-for-California-Map-207765-1.html |url-status=dead |archive-date=January 6, 2012 |title=Members Brace for California Map |publisher=[[Roll Call]] |date=July 27, 2011 |access-date=July 27, 2011 |first=Kyle |last=Trygstad }}</ref> was succeeded by [[Juan Vargas]].

== Incumbents defeated ==
As a result of redistricting, many incumbents were forced to compete against each other in the same district, which resulted in a larger number of incumbents being defeated in primaries.

=== In primary elections ===
Thirteen representatives lost renomination: eight were lost in redistricting battles pitting incumbents against each other, and five incumbents lost nomination to non-incumbent challengers.

==== Democrats ====
Seven Democrats lost renomination: five in redistricting and two to a non-incumbent challenger.

===== Seat held by a Democrat =====
These primary winners later won the general election.

# {{ushr|Michigan|14|X}}: [[Hansen Clarke]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[Gary Peters (Michigan politician)|Gary Peters]]
# {{ushr|Missouri|1|X}}: [[Russ Carnahan]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[Lacy Clay]]
# {{ushr|New Jersey|9|X}}: [[Steve Rothman]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[Bill Pascrell]]
# {{ushr|Ohio|9|X}}: [[Dennis Kucinich]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[Marcy Kaptur]]
# {{ushr|Pennsylvania|17|X}}: [[Tim Holden]] lost to challenger [[Matt Cartwright]]<ref name="PADemsLoss"/>
# {{ushr|Texas|16|X}}: [[Silvestre Reyes]] lost to challenger [[Beto O'Rourke]]<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2012/05/reyes-falls-hall-wins-in-texas-076848 |title=Texas primary results: Silvestre Reyes falls, Ralph Hall wins&nbsp;— Alex Isenstadt |publisher=Politico.Com |access-date=December 3, 2012}}</ref>

===== Seat lost to a Republican =====
# {{ushr|Pennsylvania|12|X}}: [[Jason Altmire]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[Mark Critz]],<ref name="PADemsLoss">{{cite news |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2012/04/26/us/politics/2-house-democrats-defeated-after-opposing-health-law.html |title=2 House Democrats Defeated After Opposing Health Law |first=Jonathan |last=Weisman |date=April 25, 2012 |access-date=April 26, 2012 |work=The New York Times}}</ref> who later lost the general election

==== Republicans ====
Six Republicans lost renomination: three in redistricting races and three to a non-incumbent challenger. All the seats were held by Republicans.
# {{ushr|Arizona|6|X}}: [[Ben Quayle]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[David Schweikert]]
# {{ushr|Florida|3|X}}: [[Cliff Stearns]] lost to challenger [[Ted Yoho]]
# {{ushr|Florida|7|X}}: [[Sandy Adams]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[John Mica]]
# {{ushr|Illinois|16|X}}: [[Don Manzullo]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[Adam Kinzinger]]
# {{ushr|Ohio|2|X}}: [[Jean Schmidt]] lost to challenger [[Brad Wenstrup]]
# {{ushr|Oklahoma|1|X}}: [[John Sullivan (Oklahoma politician)|John Sullivan]] lost to challenger [[Jim Bridenstine]]

=== In general elections ===

==== Democrats ====
Ten incumbent Democrats lost re-election; four to fellow Democrats and six to Republicans. Four losses were in California: two due to redistricting putting two incumbents together (resulting in a net loss of two for the Democrats) and two due to the state's [[nonpartisan blanket primary|top two primary]]. Two incumbents outside of California lost to Republican incumbents after being redistricted to the same district.

===== Seat held by a Democrat =====
#{{ushr|California|15|X}}: [[Pete Stark]] lost to [[Eric Swalwell]]
#{{ushr|California|30|X}}: [[Howard Berman]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[Brad Sherman]]
#{{ushr|California|35|X}}: [[Joe Baca]] lost to [[Gloria Negrete McLeod]]
#{{ushr|California|44|X}}: [[Laura Richardson]] lost a redistricting race to fellow incumbent [[Janice Hahn]]

===== Seat lost to a Republican incumbent =====
#{{ushr|Iowa|3|X}}: [[Leonard Boswell]] lost a redistricting race to [[Tom Latham (politician)|Tom Latham]]
#{{ushr|Ohio|16|X}}: [[Betty Sutton]] lost a redistricting race to [[Jim Renacci]]

===== Seat lost to a Republican challenger =====
#{{ushr|Kentucky|6|X}}: [[Ben Chandler]] lost to [[Andy Barr (American politician)|Andy Barr]].
#{{ushr|New York|27|X}}: [[Kathy Hochul]] lost to [[Chris Collins (New York politician)|Chris Collins]].
#{{ushr|North Carolina|8|X}}: [[Larry Kissell]] lost to [[Richard Hudson (North Carolina politician)|Richard Hudson]].
#{{ushr|Pennsylvania|12|X}}: [[Mark Critz]] lost to [[Keith Rothfus]].

==== Republicans ====
Seventeen incumbent Republicans lost re-election.

===== Seat held by a Republican =====
One incumbent Republican lost re-election to a fellow incumbent Republican.
#{{ushr|Louisiana|3|X}}: [[Jeff Landry]] lost to [[Charles Boustany]]

== Reapportionment ==
{{see also|United States congressional apportionment|Redistricting in the United States}}
The [[2010 United States census]] determined how many of the 435 congressional districts each state receives for the 2010 redistricting cycle. Due to population shifts, Illinois, Iowa, Louisiana, Massachusetts, Michigan, Missouri, New Jersey, and Pennsylvania each lost one seat; and New York and Ohio each lost two seats. Conversely, Arizona, Georgia, Nevada, South Carolina, Utah, and Washington each gained one seat; Florida gained two seats; and Texas gained four seats.<ref>{{cite news|url=http://2010.census.gov/news/pdf/apport2010_table1.pdf |title=Apportionment Population and Number of Representatives, By State: 2010 Census |work=US Census |date=December 21, 2010 |access-date=December 21, 2010 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20110124054026/http://2010.census.gov/news/pdf/apport2010_table1.pdf |archive-date=January 24, 2011 }}</ref>

== Newly created seats ==
Of the 435 districts created in the 2010 redistricting, nineteen had no incumbent representative.
{{col-begin}}
{{col-break}}
=== Democratic gain ===
Twelve Democrats were elected in newly created seats.
{{div col}}
#{{ushr|Arizona|1|X}}: won by [[Ann Kirkpatrick]]
#{{ushr|Arizona|9|X}}: won by [[Kyrsten Sinema]]
#{{ushr|California|29|X}}: won by [[Tony Cardenas]]
#{{ushr|California|41|X}}: won by [[Mark Takano]]
#{{ushr|California|47|X}}: won by [[Alan Lowenthal]]
#{{ushr|Florida|9|X}}: won by [[Alan Grayson]]
#{{ushr|Florida|22|X}}: won by [[Lois Frankel]]
#{{ushr|Ohio|3|X}}: won by [[Joyce Beatty]]
#{{ushr|Texas|33|X}}: won by [[Marc Veasey]]
#{{ushr|Texas|34|X}}: won by [[Filemon Vela Jr.]]
#{{ushr|Nevada|4|X}}: won by [[Steven Horsford]]
#{{ushr|Washington|10|X}}: won by [[Denny Heck]]
{{div col end}}
{{col-break}}

=== Republican gain ===
Seven Republicans were elected in newly created seats.
{{div col}}
#{{ushr|California|21|X}}: won by [[David Valadao]]
#{{ushr|Florida|6|X}}: won by [[Ron DeSantis]]
#{{ushr|Georgia|9|X}}: won by [[Doug Collins (politician)|Doug Collins]]
#{{ushr|South Carolina|7|X}}: won by [[Tom Rice]]
#{{ushr|Texas|25|X}}: won by [[Roger Williams (Texas politician)|Roger Williams]]
#{{ushr|Texas|36|X}}: won by [[Steve Stockman]]
#{{ushr|Utah|2|X}}: won by [[Chris Stewart (Utah politician)|Chris Stewart]]
{{div col end}}
{{col-end}}

== Election ratings ==
{{main|2012 United States House of Representatives election ratings}}

== Special elections ==

There were six special elections in 2012. Winners would have a [[Seniority in the United States House of Representatives|seniority advantage]] over other freshmen.  Two elections were held separate from the November elections and four elections were held concurrent with the November elections.

== Arizona ==
{{Main|2012 United States House of Representatives elections in Arizona}}
{{See also|List of United States representatives from Arizona}}
Arizona gained one seat in reapportionment. A second open seat was created when a pair of Republicans were redistricted into the same district.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://www.azsos.gov/election/2012/Primary/FullListing.htm |title=2012 Primary Election-Full Listing |publisher=[[Arizona Secretary of State]] |date=June 20, 2012 |access-date=June 22, 2012 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120619004338/http://www.azsos.gov/election/2012/Primary/FullListing.htm |archive-date=June 19, 2012 |url-status=dead |df=mdy-all }}</ref> Primary elections were August 28, 2012.

== California ==
[[File:CA2012House.svg|thumb|California's results]]
{{Main|2012 United States House of Representatives elections in California}}
{{See also|List of United States representatives from California}}
California retained its fifty-three seats: four new seats were created when four pairs of Representatives were redistricted to run against each other. An additional Republican incumbent, Gary Miller, won re-election in an entirely different district from the one he had previously represented.<ref>{{cite web |last1=Girardot |first1=Frank |title=It's Politics: Miller Hopes Move East Will Keep Him in Congress |url=http://www.sgvtribune.com/general-news/20120113/its-politics-gary-miller-hopes-move-east-will-keep-him-in-congress |publisher=San Gabriel Valley Tribune |access-date=July 21, 2014 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140729212830/http://www.sgvtribune.com/general-news/20120113/its-politics-gary-miller-hopes-move-east-will-keep-him-in-congress |archive-date=July 29, 2014 |url-status=dead |df=mdy-all }}</ref> The election featured the first use of the top-two primary system in which primary elections list candidates from all parties on one ballot, and the top two vote-getters advance to the general election. Two elections (30th and 44th districts) featured two Democratic incumbents running against each other.<ref>{{cite news |url=http://graphics.latimes.com/california-primary-june-5-2012 |title=California primary June 5, 2012 |date=July 13, 2012 |access-date=July 31, 2012 |work=Los Angeles Times}}</ref>

== Florida ==
{{Main|2012 United States House of Representatives elections in Florida}}
{{See also|List of United States representatives from Florida}}
Florida gained two seats in reapportionment. As a result of the Fair Districts Amendment, approved by voters via referendum in 2010, the legislature could not take incumbency into account in drawing the lines.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://ballotpedia.org/Florida_Congressional_District_Boundaries,_Amendment_6_(2010) |title=Florida Congressional District Boundaries, Amendment 6 (2010) |website=Ballotpedia }}</ref> As a result, two incumbent Republicans, John Mica and Sandy Adams, were drawn into the same district, creating a third new seat.<ref>{{cite book |last1=Miller |first1=William |title=The Political Battle Over Congressional Redistricting |date=2013 |publisher=Lexington Books |page=129 }}</ref>

== New York ==
{{Main|2012 United States House of Representatives elections in New York}}
{{See also|List of United States representatives from New York}}
New York lost two seats in reapportionment. After the legislature failed to reach agreement, New York conducted its 2012 congressional elections under a map drawn by a federal magistrate judge. Two incumbent Representatives saw their districts eliminated; one, Maurice Hinchey, chose to retire, while the other, Bob Turner, chose to run for the U.S. Senate.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://www.dailyfreeman.com/general-news/20120306/ny-redraw-chops-rep-maurice-hincheys-district-puts-ulster-greene-columbia-most-of-dutchess-in-chris-gibsons-district-with-maps |title=NY redraw chops Rep. Maurice Hinchey's district, puts Ulster, Greene, Columbia, most of Dutchess in Chris Gibson's district (with maps) |date=March 6, 2012 }}</ref> A third incumbent impacted by redistricting, Gary Ackerman, chose to retire, creating an open seat.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://www.nydailynews.com/blogs/dailypolitics/rep-gary-ackerman-won-seek-re-election-blog-entry-1.1689463 |title=Rep. Gary Ackerman Won't Seek Re-Election |website=NY Daily News }}</ref>

==Texas==
{{Main|2012 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas}}
{{See also|List of United States representatives from Texas}}
Texas gained four seats in reapportionment. After the initial redistricting map drawn by the Texas Legislature was denied pre-clearance by a federal district court under Section 5 of the Voting Rights Act, Texas conducted its 2012 congressional elections under a court-ordered interim map.<ref>{{cite web |last1=Center |first1=Shira |title=Rick Perry Signs Texas Election Map Into Law |url=http://atr.rollcall.com/rick-perry-signs-texas-congressional-map-into-law/ |publisher=Roll Call |access-date=July 21, 2014 |date=June 26, 2013 |archive-date=July 30, 2014 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140730051941/http://atr.rollcall.com/rick-perry-signs-texas-congressional-map-into-law/ |url-status=dead }}</ref>

==Non-voting delegates==
{{Main|2012 United States House of Representatives election in American Samoa|2012 United States House of Representatives election in District of Columbia|2012 United States House of Representatives election in Guam|2012 United States House of Representatives election in Puerto Rico|2012 United States House of Representatives election in Northern Mariana Islands|2012 United States House of Representatives election in United States Virgin Islands}}
{{See also|List of Delegates to the United States House of Representatives from American Samoa|List of Delegates to the United States House of Representatives from the District of Columbia|List of Delegates to the United States House of Representatives from Guam|List of Delegates to the United States House of Representatives from the United States Virgin Islands|List of Resident Commissioners of Puerto Rico|United States congressional delegations from the Northern Mariana Islands}}
Puerto Rico's Resident Commissioner is elected to a four-year term during U.S. presidential election years. It is the only seat in the House elected for a four-year term.
